## 3. What is IDP in Databricks?
**Channel:** Alex The Analyst | **Views:** 3K | **Date:** 2 weeks ago | **Duration:** 8:07 | **ID:** ItzSyfNEptk
**Link:** https://youtube.com/watch?v=ItzSyfNEptk

### Transcript:
What's going on everybody? Welcome back to another video. Today we're going to be talking about IDP and data [music] bricks. Now, as a data analyst, I spend a lot of my time on data collection. So, that's going to be getting the data into a usable format for other teams. And a lot of the times it's just in a CSV or it's in a database. It's really easy to use, but it isn't always the case. In fact, a lot of the data that I used to work with was in unstructured formats. Specifically, this was in healthcare when we were working directly with hospitals. And so, we would get doctor's notes, we would get clinician notes, we would get claims documents, and that data is not formatted in columns and rows. It's just free text. And I'm old enough to remember we would use OCR to extract all this data and make it usable and put it into columns and rows. That process was very difficult, filled with a ton of rules and things that would break down a lot, but I don't think that's the way that you should be doing it anymore. In fact, I've been using IDP in data bricks and it is amazing. So, let's dive into IDP and see what it is and why it is so useful. The first thing to look at is just what IDP actually is. It stands for intelligent document processing. It just uses AI to extract data and information from your documents. And I'm sure a lot of you guys have actually tried this at some point. You just pop it into Chat GBT. You say, "Hey, tell me what this document says." And it gives you kind of a right answer. I've done that myself. And for the most part, it's okay, but it's not amazing. You can even see here on the lefth hand side, this is a benchmark test for this type of process. This is going to be accuracy versus pricing. And you can see cha JBT way on the right hand side. It's going to be quite expensive for just okay accuracy right around 76 77%. You'll notice though data bricks is in the top left which is kind of exactly where you want to be. You want it to be the most accurate for the lowest price and data bicks does that. Now why does IDP matter at all? And this goes back to the exact thing I was talking about at the very beginning, which is a lot of data is not actually stored in databases and columns and rows. It's just free text. It's unstructured data. There is a ton of really important data in these unstructured data types and just these free text and documents that are sitting out there that people want to be able to use, but they just can't in its current state. Something that makes IDP really useful is that it is taking into context the document that you're processing. So, it isn't fully rules-based, which OCR pretty much was. When I was back on my data collection team, we had a lot of pipelines that had OCR in it. And I would say at least once a week, they would break. They would just break down because the structure changed or something changed in the documents or the document file type changed. It just would happen all the time. This is what makes IDP really powerful because it isn't completely rules-based like OCR was because if anything changed within that document, it just broke. it just immediately stopped working and it was horrible and we had to fix things all the time. But with IDP, it's really flexible to the document that you're actually using. For example, on this right hand side, this is just a PDF document. This is some image that somebody put out there, but it isn't sitting in columns and rows. There's free text on the lefthand side. There's columns and rows on the right hand side. Now, this data might be really useful that you want to extract even the data in the free text on the lefth hand side. With IDP, you'll be able to write pretty simple code to extract the data in the free text. And it's very easy to extract the data on the right hand side. And if things change, maybe they flip sides or more data is added or there's more free text on the lefth hand side, it's still going to be really flexible with that data type. And in future lessons, we are going to deep dive into this. I'm going to actually show you how to extract data just like this. So, let's take a look at the difference between traditional processing and IDP. Now, traditional processing is the one that I kind of grew up with or the one that I've used the most because this is just what we had when we were doing this type of work. This is going to be using OCR, which stands for optical character recognition. This is what I used and basically everybody else used for unstructured data. And you would set custom rules for each document that you were working with. There were so many different tools and templates that you can use, and those were really helpful. But again, things would break fairly easily, and so it was a lot of maintenance to actually keep up with these pipelines. The most frustrating thing about using this was when a customer would not tell you that they're going to be changing a format to a PDF or whatever document they were sending you. They would just change it and you wouldn't find out until like a week later or 2 weeks later when the data just wasn't coming in anymore. Now, it was still worth maintaining. It was still worth using those OCR systems because we would get a lot of really good data out of it, but it wasn't 100% accurate. It just means thinking in an analytical way to break down problems to actually solve problems better. The second thing that we're going to do is look at the problem solving life cycle because there is kind of a structured path to problem solving if you really think about it. Let's look first at an analytical mindset. Now, some people are born this way and I know people who are born just that's how their brain works. I was not one of those people. I promise you when I first started in analytics and being a data analyst, I didn't understand how to think at all. I was like, I don't understand this problem. This does not make sense to me. So, I was definitely a person who just kind of learned this over time. And really, the best way to think about an analytical mindset is to think like a detective. You're treating a problem like it is a mystery, like it's something you need to solve, like a puzzle. And you're putting all the pieces together, and you're looking at everything that you have, and then you're trying to solve it afterwards. Some of the best people I've worked with, data engineers, software engineers, you know, data analysts, data scientists, this is how they think. They may run into an issue or get some really strange request and they have to really dig in. and they have to really figure out how to solve that problem and it just makes them happy. They're just curious about it and they love it. And I was not always that way. I used to get frustrated. I just wanted things to be easier. But it is something that over time I've gotten a lot better at. And now I treat things a lot more like a puzzle. I like building things because I've just developed that kind of skill over time. So if you're not that way by nature, you're not always thinking about this is a problem to solve. How can I best solve it? Well, don't worry because in just a little bit we're going to talk about how to develop this as a skill. Something that's very important when solving problems is connecting it back to something that actually matters for the business. And you always need to be asking why. Why? What? When? Where? Those W questions. These questions actually make a big difference. Why are we actually trying to solve this problem? Where is the data? Where is the issue? Where is the code? These are questions that as you're digging in, you need to be asking yourself and probably asking the person whoever owns this project or owns this data or whatever it is. You're asking them too. You're saying, "Okay, I know you're requesting that of me. Why am I actually doing this?" They may explain it to you and you know it kind of sounds logical, but that's when you need to start breaking it down even more. Okay, I understand what you said, but why do we have to do it this way? Why do we need to sit it here? Why is this connected to this other project? Because the more you know, the better you're going to be able to solve it. Something that's really important in problem solving in general is understanding when you just can't solve it at all. And this is just kind of a caveat to what we're going to be talking about in the next slide, which is kind of that problem solving life cycle. There are times where you just can't solve it with the data given. And this has happened to me in the past where, you know, someone will come to me and they're like, "Hey, here's what we need. Let's, you know, let's figure that out." And I'll jump right into the data and I'm trying to solve it and I'll spend a week on it. Then I realize I'm like, "We just don't have the data. This is probably something I should have been talking about or asking in the first place, but as I dug into it, we just didn't have what we actually needed. So, we needed to go back to the drawing board, see if we could solve it in a different way, see if we needed to start collecting new data, and then we were able to solve that problem further down the line. But what we currently had, we weren't able to solve it at all. I think personally, a lot of this just comes with experience. I was not born this way. And so, when I first got into my first data role, I didn't really know what to ask. I didn't think in an analytical way. But as you get into it, as you start talking to people with more experience, learning from a mentor, learning from other people you're working with, you can develop this and you kind of see how they interact, how they're asking questions, what kind of questions they're asking, and you can learn from them. I think learning from experience and learning from mentors for this specific skill is very, very relevant. Now, let's take a look at the problem solving life cycle. Now, typically what happens, and this happens to a lot of beginners, but it happens to experienced people sometimes as well, is you'll go to number one and you'll usually skip all the way to about number five. That means someone comes to you with a request. You're like, "Okay, I've defined the problem. Here's the request." And you jump right to number five of conducting the analysis and then getting your findings and providing a recommendation like as quick as possible. This has happened to me many times as well. But number two, three and four are actually a really important part of the process. So let's look at number one, which is define the problem. This is the very first step. You're getting some type of request. They are asking you to do something. You need to actually know what you're doing. So you're just defining the problem. This is when those W questions are really important. Why? When? Where? How? Who? Who has this data? Why are we doing this? Why do we need to be doing this if we have this other product? you're kind of digging in and asking these difficult questions that you know they may or may not know the answers to. But asking these questions can prompt a lot of things in the business world. Next, you need to structure the problem and prioritize issues. And these kind of can come into play. You have to start identifying the steps that you need to take in order to actually solve this problem. I need to get the data. I need to do this to the data. Then we need to do this. But in that process, there are potentially going to be other projects that you're working on, other issues that need to be solved. And so when you're thinking about these, you're going to need to prioritize the issues. Maybe the biggest issue is you actually have to get access to the data. And that is one of the first things you need to do. But maybe it's visualizing the data. But if that's the biggest issue, you need to tackle that first. But maybe there's another issue. Maybe you need to get approval from someone. Maybe you need to get a budget approved. Maybe you need to hire somebody for this, you know, large issue that you're trying to solve. Then that is the biggest issue. You have to prioritize those things which make sure that you get this problem solved as quickly and as strategically as possible. Now, the next one is to develop issue analysis. And this sounds very fancy, but really all it means is you're breaking things down into smaller chunks. It can be very difficult to kind of look at a problem in its whole. But when you start breaking it down into smaller chunks, into more bite-sized issues, right? Larger issues tend to have smaller issues within them, you can then go problem by problem, and you can solve those and then solve the entire thing, but in just a more manageable way. Step number five is probably the most exciting piece for most people. This is where your technical skills and your hard skills with all your coding languages and database knowledge comes into play. You start conducting your analysis. But notice that almost half the life cycle was preparing. And there's this really famous quote. I don't know how it goes exactly, but it's basically that if you were given an hour to chop down a tree, you'd spend 50 of those minutes sharpening your axe. Right? It's the preparation of the actual task that's going to be able to make that task a lot easier. Once you conduct your analysis, and this can be done in a hundred different ways, so I'm not going into all of it. Then you have to synthesize and kind of bring together everything that you found. And that can be very difficult because sometimes these problems are big and there's a lot of information, a lot of different places, but you have to aggregate all these things. Kind of take a look at the bigger picture. Look back and see what problem you were trying to solve because sometimes you kind of get off track. Look back and say, "This is what we were trying to solve. Here's what I found on this problem." Lastly, you're going to develop recommendations. And this could be actually creating a dashboard or creating a report or it could be an actual recommendation where you're saying, "Hey, here's what I found. Here's what I recommend for the business or for the team or for your client." The interesting thing about this is that it is cyclical. It means it's going to keep happening. And there are times where I've worked on a project and, you know, these things happen where you get done and then there's more questions and more problems and more things and the cycle just keeps going. So, it doesn't always end. Sometimes this life cycle happens once, you're done with that project, and you don't have to go back to the drawing board. But sometimes other issues pop up, other things happen, and then you have to restart this cycle all over again. Slowing down following this life cycle will save you a lot of time. There have been times in my career where I've jumped right to number five, and it ended up costing me a lot of time that just wasn't needed. Sometimes I didn't even need to do that project or solve that problem. it was already solved somewhere else. I just didn't ask the right questions. I didn't really know what I was doing. And so, take it from, you know, eight years ago. Alex, this really does work. I really hope that this was helpful to someone out there. If you want to dive even more into soft skills for data professionals, I have a full course on my platform analystbuilder.com. I will leave a link in the description if you want to check it out. Now, I have talked about this in the past on LinkedIn and other videos about how you're communicating says a lot whether you mean it or not. And maybe you're just a person who is not good at a lot of these things about eye contact and body language and all these different aspects of communication, but you can get better at them. And so, that's we're going to work on. Again, I'm going to be really, really vulnerable here and I'm going to kind of do some awkward things, but I'm hoping you can at least visualize and kind of see the difference between doing it wrong and doing it right. The first thing I want to talk about is eye contact. Now, this is one that when I was presenting, I was really bad at. Here's what I would do. So, let's say that this is the screen right here. I'm giving a presentation. You are the audience. Okay? This is what I used to do when it came to eye contact in presentation and in meetings. I used to go like this. Uh, and so this is the data. This is how we got it. We cleaned up the data. We did all these things. Uh, and that was about it. And so, uh, you know, you can tell that, you know, this process is done. I think we're done with this, uh, with this project. Any are there any questions? As you can tell, there was not much eye contact. And that doesn't give a lot of confidence in meetings, right? You kind of want to like stick out your chest a little bit. It's all about like confidence when you're in presentations and meetings, which is really tough for someone like me. But here's a little bit better way to do it. You know, you have your presentation in the same spot. So, I'm going to present and then I'm going to kind of scan the room and I'm going to be talking while I scan the room and I'm making eye contact. So, I'm talking about the data and that's how we clean the data and that's how we do things. Now that we have all this data ingested and we're ready to go, are there any questions? In the first example, I'm not making good eye contact. I'm looking mostly at my presentation and then I'm looking down. I might look up very briefly to see if people are kind of paying attention, but that is not very confident. The much more better option is kind of scanning the room and making eye contact with people while you talk, gauging what they're seeing. You can get feedback from your audience or whoever you're talking to based off of kind of looking at them and making eye contact. So, what I have learned to do is I look at my presentation, but then I make eye contact and I scan the room. I make eye contact with multiple people and then I might go back and then I'll ask, you know, are there any questions at the end? I hope you can see that that is a big difference making eye contact versus not making eye contact. That can also apply if you're talking to someone one-on-one or you're in some type of video conference. Video conferencing I found a lot easier because I'm looking at someone but I'm not like making eye contact cuz I can look in their eyes but the camera's like a little bit higher and so it's a little bit easier. But talking to someone one-on-one that was always something I kind of would like yeah that's a good point. I'd look away and kind of things like that. But over time, again, I've grown in these skills and you try to make more eye contact and kind of be more reassuring. That just helps build that connection with your audience or with the meeting that you're in. Eye contact is really important. The next thing to look at is non-verbals, body language, and tone. These are all really, really important things. These are the things that maybe you aren't even thinking about. They just kind of subconsciously happen. Let's talk about the non-verbals and body language first. A lot of books that I've read on this topic in particular will say non-verbals and body language communicate about 65 to 75% of what you're trying to say without you actually saying anything. So the words out of your mouth of course really important. But the way you say it, the body language, the non-verbals, these are all really important. And I used to be kind of a crossarm kind of person. That is just how I typically was when I was presenting or I was sitting in a meeting. I would just sit back and I just kind of be like, "Yep, yeah, got that project done. Thanks. Bye." And that was kind of like that's how I just operated. It was very comfortable for me. It's kind of a defensive position. Now, that's perfectly fine in certain situations. But when you're communicating with some people, they do pick up on these things. And so, typically when I'm communicating, I'm sitting up tall. I'm leaning forward. I might even put my hands like on my desk, kind of lean in. This is that next part, which is active listening. These are part of the non-verbals and body language. If you are giving a presentation and you're looking around the room and people's non-verbals and their body language is just they're on their phones, they're over here, you know, they're just on this thing, they're not paying attention at all. That is a non-verbal. They could also be talking to someone else. That's also non-verbal. Their body language could be very negative. Maybe they have a frown face. Their arms are crossed and, you know, they're shaking their head. they aren't communicating with words, but they are communicating a whole heck of a lot of things in that instance. So, for you personally, if you're giving some type of presentation, you're talking to someone, I recommend doing a little bit of hand movements, right? Not crazy. You don't have to be like all over the place, but some really gentle hands movements. Just kind of going like this, you know, when you're talking or going like that or you're pointing to the presentation. It's a lot better than just kind of putting your hands in your pocket and standing there like this and just um not doing anything or crossing your own arms and being like, "And so that's the data and that's what we found and that's it." And that's again the body language is very negative. Try to be more relaxed. Try to use your hands. Try to smile. These are things that communicate that you're positive, that you're confident in what you're presenting. And these are all really important things. Now, when someone else is talking to you, that active listening really comes into play. Again, I usually was just kind of a person who would sit there and just listen, right? They'd be giving their presentation. I would just kind of look and that's about it. That's all I would do. Looking back, you know, that isn't a horrible thing, right? You're actively looking at them, but the body language says a lot. So now what I do is I usually have some type of pencil and paper. Shows that I'm taking notes, shows that I'm listening, but I'm actively engaged. If they are scanning the room and kind of making eye contact like we talked about earlier, I'm gonna kind of nod my head and kind of give them like a gentle smile or something like that. That sounds weird, but you know, they're looking around the room looking for feedback. And I'm like, okay, that shows that you are actively listening. You're taking this meeting or this conversation seriously, and that really is nice to people. People want to feel heard. They want to feel like what they're saying is important. And so active listening helps show and actually demonstrate, hey, I am here. I'm listening. what you're saying is important and people really do appreciate that. The last thing to talk about is tone. And tone is so important for a lot of people. A lot of people have really bad tones and they don't know it, right? You just that's how you talk. You're like, I can't control that. That's just how I talk. And you're not wrong, right? For a lot of people, that's just how they talk. But it can be changed. I have met a lot of people who talk in just a negative tone. And it's not very enjoyable to talk with them. When I talk, I try to be positive. I try to talk kindly about things. I try to make my tone known that what I'm saying is positive if it's a positive conversation, right? If it's something super negative and like terrible, we just lost our jobs. I'm not going to be like, "Yay, that's great." Um, it's going to be a negative tone, but in the right context, when you're giving a presentation, when you're talking about certain things, when you're having a one-on-one with your boss or with a co-orker, these are times where the tone really does matter. A part of tone is also sarcasm. And sarcasm has its place. I like using sarcasm. in certain times. But sarcasm does not usually translate super well to the business world. There are a lot of people out there who just don't understand sarcasm, who don't use sarcasm. And when you use sarcasm, it's taken very incorrectly, and that typically has to do with how you're saying it, which is related to tone. So Jenny gave a presentation, the meeting's over, she comes up to you says, "Hey, Alex, how did I do?" And you're like, "You did great, Jenny." That tone implies she did not do great. Now, she may pick up on that, she may not pick up on that. Either way, not a good way to respond if she actually did a good job. Right? You want to say, "You did great, Jenny. Awesome work. You know, that was really good. I liked what you did here." The tone is just more uplifting and more positive. It is just an arguably better way to communicate. So, watch your tone. I know a lot of people struggle with this. I've worked with a lot of people. I've also, you know, back when I was mentoring a lot of people who are trying to get into data analytics, I often will tell them, "Hey, listen. I think you are great. you have the great skills, but you're going to struggle in interviews. Your tone, the way that you're communicating is not good. And so, you're going to have to change that if you want to get that job. Your body language, your tone, the way that you make eye contact, the way that you communicate says a lot about you and a lot about how you're going to do. And a lot of managers pick up on this. They know that in this role, it's going to be super communicationheavy. And so, if in the interviews you're not doing a good job communicating, they're going to be like, "That person would not do well here. We need someone with a lot better communication skills. Now, take everything I said with just a small grain of salt because every meeting, every interaction, every form of communication is going to be slightly different, but these are kind of rules of thumb that you want to use for almost all interacting or communicating in these roles. I really hope that this was helpful. I know it's a little bit weird at the end with some of the communicating effectively stuff, but these are things that I really wish I had known about when I first started, and these are things that I've made a lot of progress on throughout my career. And I hope that these things were helpful to you and you can apply them and you can get a lot better at communicating in your role. And that's kind of how version control works. It takes snapshots of changes you make except instead of a video game it's when you're coding. Say you're coding today and you take a snapshot and then in a week you take another snapshot and then another week you take another snapshot but then at the very end you realize a while back several weeks ago you did something wrong and you need to go back. Without version control, you may not be able to do that. You may not have saved your files appropriately. But with version control, you can just revert back to a previous snapshot and then you can start working from there. And it's a really great system. So it takes snapshots of changes. You can view your history. So if you take lots of snapshots, you can go back and you can see where you took those snapshots. You can also roll back your changes. So, like I said, if you've been coding for a week, a month, a year, you can go back to any of those previous snapshots. The next thing to know is that version control systems allow for collaboration. Now, with Git, it's going to be on your local machine, but when we connect it to something like GitHub, then you'll be able to collaborate with a team. People can clone your repository. But he created Git because he was running into so many issues with something called BitKeeper, which is what which is what the previous version control system was for Linux. And so Git introduced distributed version control, which allows lots of developers to work on a project simultaneously without conflicts. Again, one of the issues that BitKeeper had. The other biggest thing is that Git is really good at working with large projects and is really fast. And so if you have really large databases or really large code bases that you're working with, Git handles them extremely well. So that's who created it. That's kind of why it was created and a little bit about what it does. But let's take a look a little bit more into what Git really is. Git is a distributed version control system, a VCS. And like we talked about with version control systems at the very beginning, it allows you to track changes in your source code while you're developing things. Now, this was originally created for software development, but other teams use it too. When I was back on a data science team, we used Git and GitHub, and that was very common. So, a lot of data science, data engineering, they will use Git and GitHub in their workflow. It's not just for software development anymore. Git allows multiple developers to work on a project without overwriting each other's changes. And so what you have is you'll have this main branch that is kind of your source of truth. And then what you can do is git allows you to branch off of that and basically code alongside that main branch. And then when you're done, you can merge it back into that main codebase with reviews. And that way it doesn't overwrite someone else's code because you're not all coding on the same main branch. Why do people use Git specifically? Well, Git is one of the most popular version control software in the entire world. In fact, it comes pre-installed on Mac and Linux. On this right hand side, this is kind of a workflow of Git. Now we need to actually aggregate our data. Now we do this with summarize. Summarize allows us to use sum, median, whatever it is and do it on whatever column we choose. You'll also notice that we have this n and then in parentheses for our column name. If we were to save this to a dataf frame, then it would have n with the uh parenthesis like that. We can change that by saying count is equal to. So we're going to assign these counts and sign it the name of count. So if we run this now we have count right here. Now that is the most simple one. It's just a classic count. But let's take a look at let's say an average. So we're going to copy this. And now we're going to get the average of the salary. So we're going to say mean and mean means average uh within r. And then we're going to do annual salary. Now, if we run this, we're going to take a look at the average salaries within each department. So, within our parks department's 54,571. Health only has one person as we know. So, that's going to be 60,000. And then city management is 90,000. This is something that is really important when you're doing aggregations because right here, if you only had this information, you don't really know how many people are in each of these departments. Maybe it's only two people, maybe it's three, maybe it's 100, maybe it's 10,000. It is important to know when you're doing aggregations, especially with something like an average, how many numbers or how many data points are behind these numbers. We are actually able to add multiple. So if I put a comma there, then I go right down here and I say count of is equal to the n. I can run this. And we can always name them. This one, of course, says mean annual salary, but I could call this uh average salary is equal to and I need to find that equal to. And then when we run this in just a second, it will work. But we have other types of aggregations. We can do the minimum of our annual salary. And if we run this, it'll give us the smallest salary within each department. So we have department average salary right here, the count, and then we have our minimum salary. Here's the minimum amount that someone makes in each of these. Conversely, we can also do the maximum salary. Let's go ahead and do the max. And we're just going to keep stacking these aggregations all on the department. But this is the most that somebody makes within that department. Now, we also have another interesting one, and this is one that I think gets overlooked a lot, but that's going to be median. Median gets the exact middle point of your aggregation. So, let's put in annual salary here. And I'll explain this in just a sec. I'm actually getting a few too many of these aggregations. Let's put this into a data frame. So, I'm going to say aggregation dataf frame. We're going to assign this. And let's run this right here. And let's open it up. So, here we have our median annual salary and this says 90,000. Let's go back and let's take a look. Let's just take a look at city management. This only has two values. If it had three values, it would take the one in the middle, but we only have two. So, what it does is it takes those two middle points. It takes the average of those two middle points, which is going to be 90,000. Now, 60,000, we already know what happens there. 52,000 is the one that's interesting to us. So, let's order by the salary and then the department. So, now we have 25,000 all the way up to 90. So, we have 1 2 3 4 5 6 7. 7 is an odd number, which means it has a middle point, which is going to be four. So, if we go 1 2 3 and four, this is the middle point of our data. If we go back to this data frame, 52,000 is our median right here. Now, as I've gotten more into data analysis and working with numbers, I find myself using median a lot because average has the possibility, it doesn't always, but it has a possibility of skewing some numbers. Let's say, for example, in our data frame, we had Ron Swanson and he's making $10 million or $100 million. If we went and aggregated and just looked at the average, the average would be like $20 million or something like that. I don't know. I'm just throwing out a number, but the average would be insanely high, but only one person actually makes that much in the department. If we took a look at the median, the median is still going to be 52,000 because that's the middle point of the data. And so depending on what type of analysis you're doing, depending on what you're looking for, average and median can show you and give you different insights into the data.
